[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 16661)\nTotal output lines: 1\n\n{"content":[{"type":"text","text":"{\\"results\\":[{\\"title\\":\\"Set up a subscription with Bacs Direct Debit\\",\\"subtitle\\":\\"Learn how to create and charge for a subscription with Bacs Direct Debit.\\",\\"url\\":\\"https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/bacs-debit\\",\\"type\\":\\"docs\\",\\"content\\":\\"Set up a subscription with Bacs Direct Debit\\\\nLearn how to create and charge for a subscription with Bacs Direct Debit.\\\\nCheck out the [sample on GitHub](https://github.com/stripe-samples/checkout-single-subscription) or explore the [demo](https://checkout.stripe.dev/checkout).\\\\nUse this guide to set up a *subscription* (A Subscription represents the product details associated with the plan that your customer subscribes to. Allows you to charge the customer on a recurring basis) using [Bacs Direct Debit](https://docs.stripe.com/payments/bacs-debit) as a payment method and *Checkout* (A low-code payment integration that creates a customizable form for collecting payments. You can embed Checkout directly in your website, redirect customers to a Stripe-hosted payment page, or create a customized checkout page with Stripe Elements).\\\\n## Set up Stripe [Server-side]\\\\nUse our official libraries for access to the Stripe API from your application:\\\\n#### Ruby\\\\n## Create recurring products and prices\\\\n\\\\u003E The Prices API unifies how one-time purchases and subscriptions are modeled on Stripe. Existing integrations that don’t use the Prices API are still [supported](https://support.stripe.com/questions/prices-api-and-existing-checkout-integrations). However, some Checkout features only support Prices. See the [migration guide](https://docs.stripe.com/payments/checkout/migrating-prices) to upgrade to the Prices API.\\\\nTo use Checkout, you first need to create a *Product* (Products represent what your business sells—whether that\'s a good or a service) and a *Price* (Prices define how much and how often to charge for products. This includes how much the product costs, what currency to use, and the interval if the price is for subscriptions). Different physical goods or levels of service must be represented by products. Each product’s pricing is represented by one or more prices.\\\\nFor example, you can create a software *product* that has four *prices*: 10 USD/month, 100 USD/year, 9 GBP/month, and 90 GBP/year. This allows you to change and add prices without needing to change the details of your underlying products. You can create a product and price [through the API](https://docs.stripe.com/api/prices) or through [the Stripe Dashboard](https://dashboard.stripe.com/products).\\\\nIf your price is determined at checkout (for example, the customer sets a donation amount) or you prefer not to create prices upfront, you can create [prices inline](https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/bacs-debit#creating-prices-inline) at Checkout Session creation.\\\\n#### Dashboard\\\\nBefore you start configuring products, make sure you’re in a sandbox. Next, define the goods and services you plan to sell. To create a new product and price:\\\\n- Go to the [Products](https://dashboard.stripe.com/products) section in the Dashboard\\\\n- Click **Add product**\\\\n- Select “Recurring” when setting the price\\\\n- Configure the pricing plan\\\\nYou can define multiple pricing plans with different parameters for each recurring product. Each price has a generated ID that you can use as a reference during the checkout process.\\\\n\\\\u003E Products created in a sandbox can be copied to live mode so that you don’t need to re-create them. In the Product detail view in the Dashboard, click **Copy to live mode** on the upper right corner. You can copy the same sandbox product to live mode more than once. Each copy creates a separate live product, and subsequent updates to the sandbox product aren’t reflected in existing live copies.\\\\n#### API\\\\nTo create a basic [Product](https://docs.stripe.com/api/products) through the API, only the `name` field is required. The product `name`, `description`, and `images` that you supply are displayed to customers on Checkout.\\\\nNext, create a [Price](https://docs.stripe.com/api/prices) to define how much and how often to charge for your product. This includes how much the product costs, what currency to use, and its billing interval.\\\\n#### curl\\\\nThis price ID is how you refer to the product when you start the payment process with Checkout.\\\\n## Create a Checkout Session [Client-side] [Server-side]\\\\nAdd a checkout button to your website that calls a server-side endpoint to create a Checkout Session.\\\\n### Checkout Session parameters\\\\nSee [Create a Checkout Session](https://docs.stripe.com/api/checkout/sessions/create) for a complete list of usable parameters.\\\\nCreate a Checkout Session with the ID of an existing [Price](https://docs.stripe.com/api/prices). Make sure that mode is set to `subscription` and you pass at least one recurring price. You can add one-time prices in addition to recurring prices. After creating the Checkout Session, redirect your customer to the [URL](https://docs.stripe.com/api/checkout/sessions/object#checkout_session_object-url) returned in the response.\\\\n#### cURL\\\\nWhen your customer successfully completes their payment, they’re redirected to the `success_url`, a page on your website that informs them that their payment was successful. Make the Session ID available on your success page by including the `{CHECKOUT_SESSION_ID}` template variable in the `success_url` as in the above example.\\\\nCheckout Sessions expire 24 hours after creation by default.\\\\nFrom the [Dashboard](https://dashboard.stripe.com/settings/payment_methods), enable the payment methods you want to accept from your customers. Checkout supports [several payment methods](https://docs.stripe.com/payments/payment-methods/payment-method-support#product-support).\\\\n\\\\u003E Don’t rely on the redirect to the `success_url` alone for detecting payment initiation, because:\\\\n\\\\u003E\\\\n\\\\u003E - Malicious users could directly access the `success_url` without paying and gain access to your goods or services.\\\\n- After a successful payment, customers might close their browser tab before they’re redirected to the `success_url`.\\"},{\\"title\\":\\"Search\\",\\"subtitle\\":\\"Look up objects in your Stripe data.\\",\\"url\\":\\"https://docs.stripe.com/search\\",\\"type\\":\\"docs\\",\\"content\\":\\"Search query language\\\\n### Query structure and terminology\\\\nA query `clause` consists of a `field` followed by an `operator` followed by a `value`:\\\\n| clause   | `email:\\\\\\"amy@rocketrides.io\\\\\\"` |\\\\n| -------- | ---------------------------- |\\\\n| field    | `email`                      |\\\\n| operator | `:`                          |\\\\n| value    | `amy@rocketrides.io`         |\\\\nYou can combine up to 10 query clauses in a search by either separating them with a space, or using the `AND` or `OR` keywords (case insensitive). You can’t combine `AND` and `OR` in the same query. Furthermore, there’s no option to use parentheses to give priority to certain logic operators. By default, the API combines clauses with `AND` logic.\\\\nThe example query `email:\\\\\\"amy@rocketrides.io\\\\\\" metadata[\\\\\\"key\\\\\\"]:\\\\\\"value\\\\\\"` matches records where both the email address is amy@rocketrides.io, and the metadata in the record includes `key` with a value of `value`.\\\\n### Creating a query which doesn’t match a clause\\\\nYou can negate query clauses using a `-` character. For example, the following search returns records that don’t match the email `amy@rocketrides.io`.\\\\n`-email:\\\\\\"amy@rocketrides.io\\\\\\"`\\\\n### Field types, substring matching, and numeric comparators\\\\nEvery search field supports exact matching with a `:`. Certain fields such as `email` and `name` support substring matching. Certain other fields such as `amount` support numeric comparators like `\\\\u003E` and `\\\\u003C`.\\\\nEach field includes a type that defines the operations you can use in the field. For a full list of available fields, see [supported query fields for each resource](https://docs.stripe.com/search#supported-query-fields-for-each-resource).\\\\nUsing an unsupported operator, such as specifying greater than (`\\\\u003E`) on a string, returns an error.\\\\n| type    | operators                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |\\\\n| ------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\\\\n| token   | exact match (case insensitive)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |\\\\n| string  | exact match, substring (case insensitive)\\\\nAn exact match on a string type returns any record where that record contains all of the words from the query in the same order. For example the query `name:\\\\\\"one two three\\\\\\"` would match both a result with the name “one two three” and a result with the name “one two three four”. |\\\\n| numeric | exact match, greater than and less than                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |\\\\n### Quotes\\\\nYou must use quotation marks around string values. Quotation marks are optional for numeric values. For example:\\\\n- `currency:\\\\\\"usd\\\\\\"` means quotes are required.\\\\n- `payment_method_details.card.last4:1234` means quotes are optional.\\\\nYou can escape quotes inside of quotes with a backslash (`\\\\\\\\`).\\\\n`description:\\\\\\"the story called \\\\\\\\\\\\\\"The Sky and the Sea.\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"\\\\\\"`\\\\n### Metadata\\\\nYou can perform searches on [metadata](https://docs.stripe.com/api/metadata) that you’ve added to objects that support it.\\\\nUse the following format to construct a clause for a metadata search: `metadata[\\\\\\"\\\\u003Cfield\\\\u003E\\\\\\"]:\\\\\\"\\\\u003Cvalue\\\\u003E\\\\\\"`.\\\\nThe following clause demonstrates how to create a clause that queries for records with a donation ID of “asdf-jkl”: `metadata[\\\\\\"donation-id\\\\\\"]:\\\\\\"asdf-jkl\\\\\\"`.\\\\nYou can query for the presence of a metadata key on an object. The following clause would match all records where `donation-id` is a metadata key. `-metadata[\\\\\\"donation-id\\\\\\"]:null`\\\\n### Search Syntax\\\\nThe following table lists the syntax that you can use to construct a query.\\\\n| Syntax         | Usage                                                                   | Description                                                                   | Examples                                                                                              |\\\\n| -------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\\\\n| `:`            | `field:value`                                                           | Exact match operator (case insensitive)                                       | `currency:\\\\\\"eur\\\\\\"` returns records where the currency is exactly “EUR” in a case-insensitive comparison |\\\\n| `AND`, `and`   | `field:value1 AND field:value2`                                         | The query returns only records that match both clauses (case insensitive)     | `status:\\\\\\"active\\\\\\" AND amount:500`                                                                      |\\\\n| `OR`, `or`     | `field:value1 OR field:value2`                                          | The query returns records that match either of the clauses (case insensitive) | `currency:\\\\\\"usd\\\\\\" OR currency:\\\\\\"eur\\\\\\"`                                                                    |\\\\n| `-`            | `-field:value`                                                          | Returns records that don’t match the clause                                   | `-currency:\\\\\\"jpy\\\\\\"` returns records that aren’t in JPY                                                  |\\\\n| `NULL`, `null` | `field:null`                                                            | A special token used for field presence (case insensitive)                    | `url:null` returns records where a URL field is empty                                                 |\\\\n| `\\\\\\\\`            | `\\\\\\" \\\\\\\\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"\\\\\\"`                                                               | Escape quotes within quotes                                                   | `description:\\\\\\"the story called \\\\\\\\\\\\\\"The Sky and the Sea.\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"\\\\\\"`                                             |\\\\n| `~`            | `field~value`                                                           | Substring match operator (substrings must be a minimum of 3 characters)       | `email~\\\\\\"amy\\\\\\"` returns matches for amy@rocketrides.io and xamy                                         |\\\\n| `\\\\u003E`, `\\\\u003C`, `=`  | - `field\\\\u003Cvalue`\\\\n- `field\\\\u003Evalue`\\\\n- `field\\\\u003E=value`\\\\n- `field\\\\u003C=value` | Greater than/less than operators                                              | `amount\\\\u003E=\\\\\\"10\\\\\\"` brings up objects where the amount is 10 or greater                                    |\\"},{\\"title\\":\\"Backdate subscriptions\\",\\"subtitle\\":\\"Learn how to backdate subscriptions.\\",\\"url\\":\\"https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/backdating\\",\\"type\\":\\"docs\\",\\"content\\":\\"# Backdate subscriptions\\\\nLearn how to backdate subscriptions.\\\\nYou can backdate a subscription to bill customers for time that has already elapsed. This is often used when migrating to Stripe or for record keeping purposes. The `backdate_start_date` field specifies the subscription’s backdated start date. You also have the option to bill customers for this elapsed time and set the next billing date.\\\\n## Billing mode considerations\\\\nAs of [API version 2025-04-30](https://docs.stripe.com/changelog/basil#2025-04-30.preview), the behavior of backdating depends on your subscription’s [billing_mode](https://docs.stripe.com/api/subscription_schedules/create#create_subscription_schedule-billing_mode):\\\\n### Before\\\\nUse `billing_mode=classic` to create a single prorated line item for the backdated period.\\\\n### After\\\\nUse `billing_mode=flexible` to generate one line item for each natural billing period that occurs during the backdated period, which behaves similarly to regular billing.\\\\n\\\\u003E Backdating isn’t supported when an invoice has more than 250 line items, which is the default limit.\\\\n## Backdating and charging users\\\\nSometimes users can have access to your service before you create a subscription for them, and you want to charge them for that access.\\\\n#### Dashboard\\\\nTo charge users for this time through the Dashboard:\\\\n1. Go to the **Payments** \\\\u003E **Subscriptions** page.\\\\n1. Click **+Create subscription**.\\\\n1. Find or add a customer.\\\\n1. Enter the pricing and product information.\\\\n1. In the **Subscription details** section, select the start and end dates of the subscription. To backdate it, select a start date in the past.\\\\n1. Select the date that you want to start the billing period on.\\\\n1. (Optional) Set the **Payment** or **Advanced** options.\\\\n1. Click **Create subscription**. The subscription starts on the date you selected.\\\\n#### API\\\\nTo charge for backdated time using the API, create a backdated subscription by passing the `customer`, `price`, and `backdate_start_date`.\\\\nThis creates an *invoice* (Invoices are statements of amounts owed by a customer. They track the status of payments from draft through paid or otherwise finalized. Subscriptions automatically generate invoices, or you can manually create a one-off invoice) with charges for the time between the backdated start date and the current time.\\\\nIf a subscription has `billing_mode=classic`, Stripe creates a single prorated amount for the backdated period based on an imagined interval starting from the backdated start date. For example, if you have a monthly billing period beginning on the first of the month and set `backdate_start_date` to February 15 (in a non-leap year), Stripe calculates the proration based on an imagined month running from February 15 to March 15. Because February has 28 days, the prorated amount for the 14 days from February 15 to March 1 is exactly half the amount of a normal monthly charge.\\\\nSimilarly, if you set the `backdate_start_date` to January 15, Stripe bases the proration calculation on an imagined month running from January 15 to February 15. That imagined month has 31 days, so the prorated amount for the 17 days from January 15 to February 1 is 17 divided by 31 (approximately 0.548) of a normal monthly charge.\\\\nYou can also view the calculation by considering the backdated start date as the original start date. The beginning of the first full billing period then becomes the updated start date.\\\\nIf a subscription has `billing_mode=flexible`, Stripe generates separate line items for each natural billing period that occurred during the backdated period. This means that each cycle is treated as if Stripe billed it normally during that time.\\\\n## Backdating without charging users\\\\nYou can also backdate a subscription without charging the customer for the backdated period, which you can use if you’re migrating to Stripe. To do so, pass `proration_behavior: \'none\'` when you create the subscription. That sets the `start_date` to the same value as `backdate_start_date`, but it doesn’t charge the customer for backdated time.\\\\n\\\\u003E If you use `proration_behavior: \'none\'` with `billing_mode=flexible`, no invoice is generated for the backdated billing periods. Because flexible billing mode calculates credit prorations based on previously debited amounts, having no debit for the backdated periods means that subsequent subscription updates or cancellations don’t generate credit prorations for that time. This differs from classic billing mode, which calculates credit prorations independently of prior debits. For details, see [Compare classic and flexible billing mode](https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/billing-mode/compare#subsequent-proration-behavior-after-backdating-without-an-initial-charge).\\\\n## Backdating and setting the billing cycle anchor\\\\nYou can combine `backdate_start_date` with `billing_cycle_anchor` to backdate a subscription and set the billing cycle anchor to a date in the future. This creates a prorated item on the next invoice for the time between the backdated start date and the billing cycle anchor. You can use this if you’re migrating to Stripe and need to carry over the next billing date for your subscriptions while billing customers for elapsed time.\\\\nFor example, say it’s October 15 and you’re migrating to Stripe. You have a subscription that started on September 1, and the next billing date is November 1. To migrate that subscription, create a new subscription and set `backdate_start_date` to September 1 and `billing_cycle_anchor` to November 1.\\\\nThis immediately issues an invoice for the prorated amount covering the time between September 1 and November 1. It also sets the `start_date` of the sub…6661 tokens truncated… Sea.\\\\\\"\\"`                                             |\\n| `~`            | `field~value`                                                           | Substring match operator (substrings must be a minimum of 3 characters)       | `email~\\"amy\\"` returns matches for amy@rocketrides.io and xamy                                         |\\n| `>`, `<`, `=`  | - `field<value`\\n- `field>value`\\n- `field>=value`\\n- `field<=value` | Greater than/less than operators                                              | `amount>=\\"10\\"` brings up objects where the amount is 10 or greater                                    |"},{"title":"Backdate subscriptions","subtitle":"Learn how to backdate subscriptions.","url":"https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/backdating","type":"docs","content":"# Backdate subscriptions\\nLearn how to backdate subscriptions.\\nYou can backdate a subscription to bill customers for time that has already elapsed. This is often used when migrating to Stripe or for record keeping purposes. The `backdate_start_date` field specifies the subscription’s backdated start date. You also have the option to bill customers for this elapsed time and set the next billing date.\\n## Billing mode considerations\\nAs of [API version 2025-04-30](https://docs.stripe.com/changelog/basil#2025-04-30.preview), the behavior of backdating depends on your subscription’s [billing_mode](https://docs.stripe.com/api/subscription_schedules/create#create_subscription_schedule-billing_mode):\\n### Before\\nUse `billing_mode=classic` to create a single prorated line item for the backdated period.\\n### After\\nUse `billing_mode=flexible` to generate one line item for each natural billing period that occurs during the backdated period, which behaves similarly to regular billing.\\n> Backdating isn’t supported when an invoice has more than 250 line items, which is the default limit.\\n## Backdating and charging users\\nSometimes users can have access to your service before you create a subscription for them, and you want to charge them for that access.\\n#### Dashboard\\nTo charge users for this time through the Dashboard:\\n1. Go to the **Payments** > **Subscriptions** page.\\n1. Click **+Create subscription**.\\n1. Find or add a customer.\\n1. Enter the pricing and product information.\\n1. In the **Subscription details** section, select the start and end dates of the subscription. To backdate it, select a start date in the past.\\n1. Select the date that you want to start the billing period on.\\n1. (Optional) Set the **Payment** or **Advanced** options.\\n1. Click **Create subscription**. The subscription starts on the date you selected.\\n#### API\\nTo charge for backdated time using the API, create a backdated subscription by passing the `customer`, `price`, and `backdate_start_date`.\\nThis creates an *invoice* (Invoices are statements of amounts owed by a customer. They track the status of payments from draft through paid or otherwise finalized. Subscriptions automatically generate invoices, or you can manually create a one-off invoice) with charges for the time between the backdated start date and the current time.\\nIf a subscription has `billing_mode=classic`, Stripe creates a single prorated amount for the backdated period based on an imagined interval starting from the backdated start date. For example, if you have a monthly billing period beginning on the first of the month and set `backdate_start_date` to February 15 (in a non-leap year), Stripe calculates the proration based on an imagined month running from February 15 to March 15. Because February has 28 days, the prorated amount for the 14 days from February 15 to March 1 is exactly half the amount of a normal monthly charge.\\nSimilarly, if you set the `backdate_start_date` to January 15, Stripe bases the proration calculation on an imagined month running from January 15 to February 15. That imagined month has 31 days, so the prorated amount for the 17 days from January 15 to February 1 is 17 divided by 31 (approximately 0.548) of a normal monthly charge.\\nYou can also view the calculation by considering the backdated start date as the original start date. The beginning of the first full billing period then becomes the updated start date.\\nIf a subscription has `billing_mode=flexible`, Stripe generates separate line items for each natural billing period that occurred during the backdated period. This means that each cycle is treated as if Stripe billed it normally during that time.\\n## Backdating without charging users\\nYou can also backdate a subscription without charging the customer for the backdated period, which you can use if you’re migrating to Stripe. To do so, pass `proration_behavior: \'none\'` when you create the subscription. That sets the `start_date` to the same value as `backdate_start_date`, but it doesn’t charge the customer for backdated time.\\n> If you use `proration_behavior: \'none\'` with `billing_mode=flexible`, no invoice is generated for the backdated billing periods. Because flexible billing mode calculates credit prorations based on previously debited amounts, having no debit for the backdated periods means that subsequent subscription updates or cancellations don’t generate credit prorations for that time. This differs from classic billing mode, which calculates credit prorations independently of prior debits. For details, see [Compare classic and flexible billing mode](https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/billing-mode/compare#subsequent-proration-behavior-after-backdating-without-an-initial-charge).\\n## Backdating and setting the billing cycle anchor\\nYou can combine `backdate_start_date` with `billing_cycle_anchor` to backdate a subscription and set the billing cycle anchor to a date in the future. This creates a prorated item on the next invoice for the time between the backdated start date and the billing cycle anchor. You can use this if you’re migrating to Stripe and need to carry over the next billing date for your subscriptions while billing customers for elapsed time.\\nFor example, say it’s October 15 and you’re migrating to Stripe. You have a subscription that started on September 1, and the next billing date is November 1. To migrate that subscription, create a new subscription and set `backdate_start_date` to September 1 and `billing_cycle_anchor` to November 1.\\nThis immediately issues an invoice for the prorated amount covering the time between September 1 and November 1. It also sets the `start_date` of the subscription to September 1. Stripe issues the next invoice on November 1.\\n## Backdating and discounts\\nWhen you apply a [coupon](https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/coupons) to a backdated subscription, the coupon’s duration starts counting from the backdated start date, not from the date you make the API call. This means the coupon’s duration is consumed by the backdated period.\\nFor example, if you create a subscription on March 1 with `backdate_start_date` set to January 1 and apply a coupon with `duration=repeating` and `duration_in_months=2`, the coupon applies to the January and February billing periods (the 2 months starting from January 1). Since the coupon’s 2-month duration has been fully consumed by the backdated period, it doesn’t apply to the March invoice or any future invoices, and the discount is removed from the subscription item.\\nIf you want the coupon to apply to current and future invoices after the backdated period, the coupon’s duration must be longer than the backdated period. Using the same example, a coupon with `duration_in_months=3` would cover January, February, and March, applying the discount to the first invoice after the backdated period.\\n> If a coupon’s duration is shorter than or equal to the length of the backdated period, the discount only applies to invoices within the backdated period. It won’t carry forward to any new invoices after the backdate.\\nThe following table summarizes how coupon duration interacts with backdating:\\n| Coupon duration        | Behavior with backdating                                                                                                                                        |\\n| ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\\n| `once`                 | Applies only to the first invoice, which covers the backdated period.                                                                                           |\\n| `repeating` (N months) | Duration starts from the backdated start date. If N is less than or equal to the backdated period, the discount expires before any future invoices are created. |\\n| `forever`              | Applies to all invoices, including those in the backdated period and all future invoices. No special considerations.                                            |\\n## Backdating an update\\nTo set the effective date of [prorations](https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/prorations) when updating a subscription, use the [proration_date](https://docs.stripe.com/api/subscriptions/update#update_subscription-proration_date) parameter. Pass an integer Unix timestamp that occurs within the current period of the subscription’s items. If the subscription uses a subscription schedule, make sure that the timestamp is before the start date of the next phase of the schedule.\\nYou can set the `proration_date` earlier than the current period only during the first period of a backdated subscription. In this situation, the `proration_date` must be on or after the `subscription[start_date]` (the backdated start date). In all other cases, the `proration_date` can’t be earlier than the `current_period_start`."},{"title":"Revenue Recognition reports in Sigma and SDP","subtitle":"Run prebuilt Revenue Recognition reports or build custom queries in Sigma.","url":"https://docs.stripe.com/revenue-recognition/reports/sigma-and-sdp","type":"docs","content":"# Revenue Recognition reports in Sigma and SDP\\nRun prebuilt Revenue Recognition reports or build custom queries in Sigma.\\nRevenue Recognition provides prebuilt report templates in [Sigma](https://docs.stripe.com/data/how-sigma-works) that you can run directly or use as a starting point for custom queries. These templates are available for both Sigma and Sigma for Organizations.\\n## Run a prebuilt template report\\nTo run a prebuilt Revenue Recognition report, navigate to [Sigma](https://dashboard.stripe.com/sigma/queries) in the Stripe Dashboard and search for the template by name. The following report templates are available:\\n- **Period summary**: Revenue, deferred revenue, and future scheduled billings activity for a given month.\\n- **Income statement**: Detailed revenue and contra revenue breakdown grouped by account.\\n- **Trial balance**: Starting balance, net change, and ending balance for all accounts.\\n- **Balance sheet**: Assets, liabilities, and equity balances for a given period.\\n- **Revenue waterfall**: How revenue from each billing period is recognized across accounting periods.\\n- **Debits and credits**: Detailed double-entry journal of all revenue recognition transactions.\\n- **AR aging report**: Outstanding accounts receivable amounts by age buckets.\\nEach of these reports has additional variants grouped by customer, product, price, invoice, or line item.\\nAll templates default to the last completed month. To change the time frame, modify the `accounting_period_date` filter in the query’s `WHERE` clause. For example, the default filter looks like this:\\nTo query a specific month, such as January 2025, replace it with:\\nTo query a full quarter, such as Q1 2025:\\n## Build a custom query\\nYou can build custom reports using the `revenue_recognition_debits_and_credits`, `revenue_recognition_month_summary`, and other tables in Sigma.\\n### Recognized revenue from unpaid invoices\\nThis query generates a report for revenue recognized from unpaid open invoices, grouped by invoice ID. You can adjust the time frame and grouping parameters.\\n> If you’re using our [chart of accounts](https://docs.stripe.com/revenue-recognition/chart-of-accounts) beta feature, be sure to update the `debit` and `credit` mappings in the query below to reflect the accounts in your general ledger."},{"title":"Track a payment link","subtitle":"Use URL parameters and UTM codes to track a payment link.","url":"https://docs.stripe.com/payment-links/url-parameters","type":"docs","content":"# Track a payment link\\nUse URL parameters and UTM codes to track a payment link.\\nModify your payment link with URL parameters and [Urchin Tracking Module (UTM)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTM_parameters) codes to learn about customer behaviors and your marketing strategy’s effectiveness. These tools help identify the source of your traffic and the marketing campaigns leading to the most conversions.\\n## Track campaigns with UTM codes\\nUse UTM codes to track how customers find your site when they pay using your payment link. You can add the following UTM codes as parameters in the query string of your URL: `utm_source`, `utm_content`, `utm_medium`, `utm_term`, and `utm_campaign`.\\n| Parameter      | Description                                                                                                                         |\\n| -------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\\n| `utm_source`   | Identifies where the traffic originated (for example, a website name, social media, or a search engine).                            |\\n| `utm_content`  | Identifies what content your customer chooses. Use this parameter to distinguish between links that point to the same payment page. |\\n| `utm_medium`   | Identifies the marketing medium that accesses your payment link (for example, email, cost per click (cpc), or other methods).       |\\n| `utm_term`     | Identifies specific search terms and keywords in your paid search ads.                                                              |\\n| `utm_campaign` | Identifies your marketing campaigns using the payment link URL.                                                                     |\\nTo add UTM codes, specify `redirect` as your [confirmation behavior](https://docs.stripe.com/payment-links/post-payment#change-confirmation-behavior). When customers complete a payment, your redirect URL contains the UTM code parameters specified in your payment link URL. Here’s how a payment link looks with an appended UTM code: **https://buy.stripe.com/test\\\\_eVa5nPg1j1wmfXq5kr?utm\\\\_medium=earned\\\\_email&utm\\\\_source=marketo&utm\\\\_campaign=campaign\\\\_a**.\\n> Construct UTM codes using alphanumeric characters, dashes, or underscores, ensuring they don’t exceed a 150-character limit. Invalid values are discreetly discarded, guaranteeing your payment links performance remains unaffected.\\n## Simplify reconciliation with a URL parameter\\nYou can simplify reconciliation with the `client_reference_id` URL parameter. Use URL parameters in the query string of your payment link URL. To configure URL parameters directly in the Dashboard [Payment Links](https://dashboard.stripe.com/payment-links) page:\\n1. Click the payment link you want to modify.\\n1. Click the down arrow of the **Copy** button and select **URL parameters**.\\n1. In the dialog, use the drop-down menu to select **Client reference ID**.\\n1. Enter a value that meets the requirements described in the following table to append the reference to your URL.\\n1. Copy the amended URL for use in your integration.\\n| Parameter             | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Syntax                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |\\n| --------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\\n| `client_reference_id` | Use `client_reference_id` to attach a unique string of your choice to the Checkout Session. This can be a customer ID or a cart ID (or similar), and you can use it to reconcile the Session with your internal systems. If you add this parameter to your payment link, it’s sent in the [checkout.session.completed](https://docs.stripe.com/api/events/types#event_types-checkout.session.completed) *webhook* (A webhook is a real-time push notification sent to your application as a JSON payload through HTTPS requests) after payment completion. | `client_reference_id` can be composed of alphanumeric characters, dashes, or underscores, and be any value up to 200 characters. Invalid values are silently dropped, but your payment page continues to work as expected.\\nDepending on how you share your Payment Links, they might show up in unexpected places. If you add a client reference ID to a payment link, make sure that it doesn’t include sensitive information or secrets such as passwords or API keys. Only share Payment Links that have URL parameters with intended recipients. |\\n## Track payments with the API\\nWhen customers use a payment link to complete a payment, Stripe sends a [checkout.session.completed](https://docs.stripe.com/api/events/types#event_types-checkout.session.completed) webhook that you can use for fulfillment and reconciliation. Learn more about [receiving Stripe events in your integration](https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks).\\nMake sure to listen to additional webhooks in case you’ve enabled payment methods like bank debits or vouchers, which can take 2-14 days to confirm the payment. For more information, see our guide on [fulfilling orders after a customer pays](https://docs.stripe.com/checkout/fulfillment).\\nAfter a customer completes a purchase, you can redirect them to a URL or display a custom message by setting [after_completion](https://docs.stripe.com/api/payment-link/create#create_payment_link-after_completion) on the payment link."}]},"isError":false}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"ddffa9","wall_time_seconds":0.0000052,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1348,"output":"---\\r\\nname: booking-link\\r\\ndescription: Use whenever Joshua wants a one-off booking link for LSAT U — \\"make me a link for Dakota at 7:15 tomorrow\\", \\"send her a link to book tonight\\", \\"one-off link, $60\\", \\"short notice link\\", \\"I need a link she can schedule herself with\\". Mints a single-use URL that sells exactly one session at a price you set, optionally pinned to one exact time, and closes after she books. Also covers listing and revoking existing links.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# One-off booking links\\r\\n\\r\\nA URL Joshua sends to one person. She opens it, books one session, pays, and\\r\\ngets the Google Meet invite from the ordinary booking flow. Then the link\\r\\ncloses.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Authorization\\r\\n\\r\\nMinting a link is pre-authorized. Joshua asking for a link means make it now —\\r\\ndo not ask permission, ask only for the facts you actually need.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Never send the link anywhere.** Print it and hand it to Joshua. He decides who\\r\\ngets it. A link is a Bearer [REDACTED]: whoever holds it can book that slot.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What you need before running\\r\\n\\r\\nOnly two things are required, and one has a default:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Price** in dollars. Required — links always charge, and the standard rate is\\r\\n  $45 but a given person may pay something else. Ask if it was not said.\\r\\n- **Who it is for.** Used only for the admin-side note, never in the URL. If\\r\\n  Joshua did not say, use a short description.\\r\\n\\r\\nEverything else is optional. Ask for nothing you were not given.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Running it\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom `~/ai-os/projects/lsatu`:\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nnpm run booking:link -- --for \\"Dakota\\" --price 45 --at \\"2026-09-10 19:15\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nTimes in `--at` are wall-clock in Joshua\'s booking timezone (`America/Toronto`),\\r\\nformat `YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm`, 24-hour. Convert \\"7:15pm tomorrow\\" yourself — resolve\\r\\nrelative dates against today before running, and never pass a relative word\\r\\nthrough.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt prints the URL, the price, the time, and how many times the link can sell.\\r\\nGive Joshua the URL exactly as printed.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The flags\\r\\n\\r\\n| Flag | Default | What it does |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| `--for` | — | Who the link is for. Note only, never in the URL. |\\r\\n| `--price` | — | Dollars. Required. |\\r\\n| `--at` | none | Pin one exact time. See below. |\\r\\n| `--minutes` | 70 | Session length. |\\r\\n| `--notice` | 1440, or 0 with `--at` | Minimum notice in minutes. `0` means \\"bookable right now\\". |\\r\\n| `--window` | 6 | How many business days ahead the picker reaches. |\\r\\n| `--label` | \\"LSAT Session\\" | The title she sees on the page. |\\r\\n| `--note` | auto | Admin reminder of who and why. |\\r\\n\\r\\n### Pinned versus picker\\r\\n\\r\\n**`--at` pins one time.** She sees only that time and confirms it. The minimum\\r\\nnotice, the rolling window and the weekly hours are all skipped, because Joshua\\r\\nchoosing the time *is* that decision. This is the right shape for \\"I have a gap\\r\\nat 7:15 tonight, let her take it.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n**Without `--at` she gets a calendar** of open times, still inside the weekly\\r\\nhours. Use `--notice 0` for a short-notice link she can book today, and\\r\\n`--window` to control how far ahead it reaches.\\r\\n\\r\\nDouble-booking is never skipped either way. A pinned time that clashes with\\r\\nsomething already on the calendar is refused at mint time — the script deletes\\r\\nthe link and tells you what it hit — and refused again inside the booking\\r\\ntransaction.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Listing and revoking\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nnpm run booking:link -- --list\\r\\nnpm run booking:link -- --revoke <slug>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n`--list` shows every one-off link with its state: `open`, `used`, or `revoked`.\\r\\nRevoking is immediate and the URL stops resolving.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reading the outcome honestly\\r\\n\\r\\n- **\\"clashes with something already on your calendar\\"** — the time is taken. No\\r\\n  link was created. Tell Joshua what he asked for is not free and offer to pick\\r\\n  another time; do not silently mint a different one.\\r\\n- **\\"would have no open times at all\\"** — a picker link whose window and weekly\\r\\n  hours produce nothing. Usually `--window` is too small or the hours are empty.\\r\\n- **A time in the past** is refused. If Joshua asks for a time that has already\\r\\n  gone by today, say so rather than rolling it to tomorrow on your own.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How it works, so you can debug it\\r\\n\\r\\nA one-off link is a row in `booking_event_types` with a random 10-character slug\\r\\nand `single_use` set. That is the same table defining the ordinary session and\\r\\nthe consultation, and every layer beneath — the page, the `single-session` edge\\r\\nfunction, the SQL slot policy, the claim transaction — is already keyed by event\\r\\nslug. So the link books through exactly the code a normal session books through.\\r\\nThere is no second booking path to keep in sync.\\r\\n\\r\\nSingle use is enforced by the `enforce_booking_event_shape` trigger under a\\r\\nper-slug advisory lock, so two people opening the same link at the same moment\\r\\ncannot both get through. A hold holds the link; letting the checkout expire\\r\\nreleases it; a cancelled or refunded booking stays spent, and a replacement\\r\\nneeds a fresh link.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelevant files:\\r\\n\\r\\n- `scripts/booking-link.ts` — the CLI\\r\\n- `src/pages/OneOffBooking.tsx` and the `/book/x/:slug` route in `src/App.tsx`\\r\\n- `supabase/migrations/20260908120000_one_off_booking_links.sql`\\r\\n- `supabase/functions/single-session/index.ts`\\r\\n\\r\\n## Credentials\\r\\n\\r\\n`LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL` / `LSATPREP_ADMIN_PASSWORD` from `~/ai-os/.env`, which is\\r\\ngit-ignored. This repo\'s `.env` is tracked in git — never put a credential\\r\\nthere.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

## assistant

Zeina’s email shows she chose **Option 1, the $45 single session**, and tried the [Cal.com booking page](https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/session-link). The two Stripe links in your email were for 5- and 10-session packages; she didn’t say she chose either.

The booking page is live and displays the $45 price and available times later in the week. When I checked, it showed no slots for **September 14** and availability beginning September 15. Since Zeina wrote on September 13 that she was trying to book “tomorrow,” the likeliest issue was **no availability for the specific day she selected**, possibly because of the booking notice window. I couldn’t verify that exact Cal.com setting.

Stripe returned no customer record for her email. That doesn’t rule out an incomplete payment attempt, but it points to the problem happening at the Cal.com availability step, before Stripe checkout. I didn’t start a checkout, since that could create a temporary booking hold.

Use when Joshua receives a direct message, Facebook/Messenger message, Superprof/TutorOcean inquiry, Reddit DM, email inquiry, or asks to \\"make a response\\" to a prospective LSAT student asking whether tutoring is available, how to start, or how to book.\\n---\\n\\n# Inbound Tutoring Replies\\n\\nUse this skill for direct inbound leads who have already shown interest in LSAT tutoring.\\n\\n## Default Rules\\n\\n- Be warm, direct, and human.\\n- Keep the reply short. Do not sound like a sales page.\\n- Do not mention price unless the person asks about pricing, rates, or cost.\\n- Mention the free first session when useful.\\n- Prefer moving the lead toward a consultation link rather than over-explaining.\\n- Include `contact@aspiringattorneys.com` as an alternate contact path when helpful.\\n- Use `LSAT`, not `lsat`, unless quoting the lead.\\n- Do not overuse Joshua\'s `138 -> 173` story in first direct-message replies unless the lead asks about background or credibility.\\n\\n## Core Reply\\n\\nUse this as the default response to: \\"Are you still offering LSAT tutoring?\\"\\n\\n```text\\nHi [Name], yes, I am still offering LSAT tutoring.\\n\\nI\'d be happy to talk and see if I can help. I usually start with a free first session so we can go over where you are scoring, your timeline, and what has been giving you the most trouble.\\n\\nYou can book a time here if you want to chat: https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/consultation\\n\\nYou can also email me at contact@aspiringattorneys.com if you have any further questions.\\n```\\n\\n## If They Ask About Price\\n\\nOnly include this if they ask about price, rates, cost, or affordability.\\n\\n```text\\nAfter the free first session, I charge $40/h.\\n```\\n\\n## If They Give Score And Test Date\\n\\nAdd one tailored sentence before the booking link. Keep it specific but not diagnostic-heavy.\\n\\nExamples:\\n\\n- `If you are around the mid-160s, the work is usually less about relearning the basics and more about tightening the few decisions that keep repeating.`\\n- `If timing is the main issue, I would want to look at where the time is actually going: stimulus read, answer choice comparison, or review habits.`\\n- `If you are still building foundations, I would focus first on question stems, stimulus structure, and a simple review system.`\\n\\n## Logging\\n\\nWhen working inside the Obsidian vault, save useful new inbound-message patterns to `wiki/knowledge/tutoring-outreach-log.md` under `Inbound message patterns`.\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 7.2 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'RESULT 1\n---\ntitle: Teaching Voice\ntype: concept\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\ncreated: 2026-06-10\nupdated: 2026-07-18\nstatus: active\nsubject: Teaching Voice\naliases: []\n---\n\n# Teaching Voice\n\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\n\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools "preserve verbatim" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\n\n## Core voice (every register)\n\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\n\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not "great job."\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard ("expect this to be exhausting and very challenging"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: "partner with you on your LSAT journey," "getting set up," "foundational." The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Short declaratives plus one slightly longer warm beat. Air between ideas. Each idea gets its own line. Real messages are short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters).\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype ("unlock," "crush," "level up," "game-changer" — "crush" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers ("Sorry for such a long delay!"). He is not exclamation-averse.\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\n\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\n\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\n\n- **Greeting.** "Hi [Name]," is the default (used ~519x). "Hey [Name]," for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). "Hello [Name]," (~105x) and "Dear [Name]," (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\n- **Sign-off.** "Best," and "Best regards," are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). "Thanks," when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). "Warmly," / "Warm regards," (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. "Talk soon," (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **"Joshua"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** "let me know" (by far the most common, ~390x), "looking forward," "I hope" / "hope all is well" / "hope you," "no worries at all," "happy to" / "more than happy to," "just wanted to make sure," "make sure," "no rush," "of course."\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs — never.)\n\n## The apology / accountability move\n\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\n\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\n\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\n\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\n\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\n\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\n\n> Hi Sandra,\n>\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\n>\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\n>\n> Your link is the same one as before:\n> [link]\n>\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\n>\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\n>\n> Once again, my apologies.\n>\n> Best,\n> Joshua\n\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** "I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours." The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** "To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet." Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then "That is fixed now."** No mechanics, no system names.\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, "pick any time it shows and it will book," and the offer to do it by hand: "If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself."\n5. **Invite the next report:** "If you run into any other issues, let me know."\n6. **Close with "Once again, my apologies."** then "Best," and "Joshua".\n\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\n- "Sorry for the runaround" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\n- "You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after." Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\n- "You have all 10 sessions" / "nothing was used from your package." Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\n\n## Situational micro-templates\n\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\n\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\n\n## The three registers\n\nWhich voice governs which surface.\n\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, recaps, in-drill feedback, email\n\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\n\nReference line (target tone):\n\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\n\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.\n  > Missing this is expected. It\'s one of the hardest moves on the test. Let\'s slow down and see where it slipped.\n- **Accountability (student isn\'t doing the work):** gentle nudge, low pressure. Frame it as a slip, find a rhythm together. Never "the reps aren\'t happening," never tough love.\n- **Praise (student nails it):** name the exact skill, tie it to their history, raise the bar.\n  > That\'s the cleanest you\'ve split premise from conclusion yet. Let\'s push it into the harder sets.\n- **Hard truth about the test:** state it plainly. "Do not expect for it to come easy. Expect to find this exhausting."\n\n### 2. Teaching content (earnest substance, wit at the edges) -- bootcamp lessons, drill explanations\n\nThe substance stays earnest and clear (you are explaining a hard skill). The wit lives only at the edges: headers, openers, transitions. No jokes mid-explanation, nothing that costs comprehension.\n\n> Header: "The flaw hides in plain sight."\n> Body: earnest, clear, step by step.\n\n### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing\n\nConfident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed "the next card is the page in front of you" was clever but empty).\n\n> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.\n\nBrand copy speaks as the platform ("we", "you"), not as an individual ("I", "Joshua"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.\n\n## Don\'t\n\n- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)\n- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging ("you might want to").\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\n- No em dashes, ever.\n\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\nthe reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.\n\nRelated: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).\n\r\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': "RESULT 2\n---\ntitle: Students — one folder per student\ntype: operating-layer\ntags: [students, operating-layer]\ncreated: 2026-07-03\nupdated: 2026-09-10\nstatus: active\nsubject: Students - one folder per student\naliases: []\n---\n\n# Students\n\nOne folder per student, holding **everything** about them (D2, locked 2026-07-16): transcripts, correspondence, homework, notes, schedules, profile. Open the student's folder and their whole working record is there. This is one of the brain's three knowledge systems, beside [[../wiki/index|wiki]] and [[../corpus/index|corpus]].\n\n## Current students\n\n- [[adriana-varea/adriana-varea|Adriana Varea]] · [[amanda/amanda|Amanda]] · [[asia/asia|Asia]] · [[avneet-natt/avneet-natt|Avneet Natt]] · [[celia-cutler/celia-cutler|Celia Cutler]] · [[charles-moore/charles-moore|Charles Moore]] · [[dakota/dakota|Dakota]] · [[david-salgado/david-salgado|David Salgado]] · [[ethan/ethan|Ethan]] · [[favour/favour|Favour]] · [[fernande-charleus/fernande-charleus|Fernande Charleus]] · [[ivory-johnson/ivory-johnson|Ivory Johnson]] · [[nicole/nicole|Nicole]] · [[ore/ore|Ore]] · [[sofia/sofia|Sofia]] · [[steven-morris/steven-morris|Steven Morris]] · [[vinay-kalva/vinay-kalva|Vinay Kalva]] · [[whit-wilson/whit-wilson|Whit Wilson]] · [[yahunatan-zetvy/yahunatan-zetvy|Yahunatan Zetvy]]\n\n**Taught in the last month** (2026-08-08 to 2026-09-08, transcripts pulled 2026-09-08): Celia Cutler (6), Adriana Varea (5), Steven Morris (4), Sofia (2), Vinay Kalva (2), Yahunatan Zetvy (2), Asia (1), Charles Moore (1), Fernande Charleus (1), Whit Wilson (1).\n\n## Former students\n\nLive in [[non-students/CLAUDE|non-students/]] as of 2026-09-10. Records preserved, excluded from every sweep. Read that folder's `CLAUDE.md` before touching anything in it.\n\n## The primary/mirror boundary (D4 — read before regenerating anything)\n\nEvery file in a student folder is one of two kinds, and the distinction decides what a rebuild may overwrite:\n\n- **Primary — the brain is the only home.** Transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, study plans, anything Joshua or an agent authored about the student. **Never regenerate, never overwrite; retain forever.**\n- **Mirror — a projection of a live backend.** Session/booking state (Cal.com + Google Calendar), homework/dossier/publish state (LSAT U Supabase), payment events (Stripe), correspondence indexes (Gmail). On any disagreement the backend wins, silently.\n\n**Convention:** mirror files MUST be named `*.generated.md` (or carry `generated: true` frontmatter). A file without that marker is primary by definition and must be treated as precious. Pre-2026-07-16 files (`email-context.md`, `payment-links.md`, `transcripts.md`) predate the marker — treat as primary until individually re-derived and renamed. No Markdown file here is ever the source of operational truth — no package balances, no running totals (all services are paid upfront; Cal.com answers sessions-used).\n\n## Standard files per student folder\n\n- `<student-slug>.md` — compact student index and routing page (primary).\n- `email-context.md` — synthesized Gmail context.\n- `email-correspondence.md` — Gmail message-id corpus for the student's confirmed address.\n- `payment-links.md` — verified payment, billing, and scheduling links.\n- `transcripts.md` — transcript and meeting-note inventory.\n- `teaching-notes.md` — durable, evidence-backed learning judgments and teaching patterns (primary).\n- `open-loops.md` — unfinished follow-ups and pending decisions (primary).\n- `live-status.generated.md` — refreshable operational summary from Calendar, Gmail, payments, and LSAT U (generated mirror).\n- `transcript-downloads/` — full local transcript exports (primary — the brain is their only home).\n- `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD-session-name/` — new self-contained session packages.\n- `assignment-log.md` — running log of assigned work and notification evidence so repeats are avoided (primary).\n- `Practice_Test_Reports/` — the student's PT score reports as received (intake form, screenshots, exports). Raw evidence — read for diagnosis, never edit.\n- `Student_Specific_Homework/` — work assigned to this student specifically. Distinct from the shared banks in `corpus/Curriculum/*/Homework/`, which are the source material it draws from.\n\n## Conventions\n\n- New student → new folder `students/<slug>/` with a `<slug>.md` profile page.\n- New session → `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD-session-name/` containing `transcript.md`, `summary.md`, and `topic-map.md`.\n- `transcript.md` preserves the raw source. `summary.md` records the auto-saved summary, action items, and appended corrections. `topic-map.md` is a timestamped link index to relevant LSAT concepts, student records, and source material.\n- Session summaries are append-only. Later corrections go in a dated correction section and never erase the original summary.\n- Generated operational state is clearly marked with `generated: true` and never overrides primary teaching records.\n- Wikilink student pages into corpus concepts and LSAT U material as they become relevant.\n- Tier A note: transcripts and correspondence are trackable (Tier-A amendment, 2026-07-16). True secrets (credentials, IDs, payment instruments) still never enter Git — `private/` only.\n\n## Operating contract\n\nFor each new consultation or transcript, Jay 2 must report what was found, what was created or updated, what was inferred, what remains missing, what is recommended, and what Joshua should confirm or reject. During the learning period, no student-facing message, assignment, or platform action is implied by record preparation alone.\n\r\n"}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'RESULT 3\nstudents\\_other-sessions\\2026-09-10-zeina-consultation-notes-by-gemini.md\n'}]

The payment link holds the slot for 30 minutes and\n// then dies; it CANNOT be re-sent (the uuid is not stored on the booking and API v2\n// has no payments endpoint). Meanwhile the dead booking keeps holding the slot.\n//\n// cal.com sends no host notification for these, so they are invisible without this check.\n//\n//   node scripts/calcom-pending-sweep.mjs           # report\n//   node scripts/calcom-pending-sweep.mjs --cancel  # also release future slots\n//\n// Needs CALCOM_API_KEY in .env. API v2 only — v1 is decommissioned.\n\nimport { readFileSync } from "node:fs";\nimport { fileURLToPath } from "node:url";\nimport { dirname, join } from "node:path";\n\nconst root = join(dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)), "..");\nconst env = Object.fromEntries(\n  readFileSync(join(root, ".env"), "utf8")\n    .split("\\n")\n    .filter((l) => l.trim() && !l.trim().startsWith("#") && l.includes("="))\n    .map((l) => [l.slice(0, l.indexOf("=")).trim(), l.slice(l.indexOf("=") + 1).trim()])\n);\n\nconst KEY = process.env.CALCOM_API_KEY || env.CALCOM_API_KEY;\nif (!KEY) { console.error("CALCOM_API_KEY missing from .env"); process.exit(1); }\n\nconst api = (path, init = {}) =>\n  fetch(`https://api.cal.com/v2${path}`, {\n    ...init,\n    headers: {\n      Authorization: [REDACTED] ${KEY}`,\n      "cal-api-version": "2024-08-13",\n      "Content-Type": "application/json",\n      ...init.headers,\n    },\n  }).then((r) => r.json());\n\nconst ET = (iso) =>\n  new Date(iso).toLocaleString("en-US", {\n    timeZone: "America/Toronto", year: "numeric", month: "short",\n    day: "numeric", hour: "numeric", minute: "2-digit",\n  });\n\nconst pending = [];\nfor (const status of ["upcoming", "past"]) {\n  const res = await api(`/bookings?status=${status}&take=100`);\n  for (const b of res.data || []) if (b.status === "pending") pending.push(b);\n}\n\nif (!pending.length) { console.log("No stuck pending bookings. Nothing holding a slot."); process.exit(0); }\n\nconst now = Date.now();\nconst future = pending.filter((b) => new Date(b.start).getTime() > now);\n\nconsole.log(`${pending.length} unpaid booking(s); ${future.length} still holding a future slot\\n`);\nfor (const b of pending) {\n  const who = (b.attendees || []).map((a) => a.email).join(", ");\n  const holding = new Date(b.start).getTime() > now ? "  << HOLDING A SLOT" : "";\n  console.log(`  ${ET(b.start).padEnd(26)} ${who.padEnd(34)} ${b.uid}${holding}`);\n}\n\nif (!process.argv.includes("--cancel")) {\n  if (future.length) console.log(`\\nRe-run with --cancel to release ${future.length} slot(s).`);\n  console.log("Past-dated ones cannot be cancelled by cal.com and block nothing — leave them.");\n  process.exit(0);\n}\n\nconsole.log("\\nCancelling future unpaid bookings:");\nfor (const b of future) {\n  const res = await api(`/bookings/${b.uid}/cancel`, {\n    method: "POST",\n    body: JSON.stringify({ cancellationReason: "Unpaid — payment window expired. Slot released." }),\n  });\n  console.log(`  ${b.uid}  ${res.status === "success" ? "released" : "FAILED: " + JSON.stringify(res.error).slice(0, 90)}`);\n}\nconsole.log("\\nStudents must rebook to get a fresh 30-minute payment link.");\n\r\n'}]

Then strike finished items through in `wiki/tasks.md` with a date, refresh `wiki/hot.md`, append a dated `wiki/log.md` entry (newest first, distinct label for same-day sessions), update the task anchor.\\r\\n\\r\\n`wiki/tasks.md` is the single source of truth for what is open vs done. Finished work is struck through so a new session never re-runs it. `raw/` in the vault is immutable.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Codex Adapter Layout\\r\\n\\r\\n- `.codex/config.toml`: minimal shared Codex project config.
